# FORENSIC LONGITUDINAL DATASET — APPLE COMPUTER COMPANY, STAGE 1

**Dataset:** THE FOUNDER'S PLAYBOOK — forensic longitudinal reconstruction, frozen universe `00_universe/`.
**Company:** Apple (internal key **company_004_apple**) — the entity that traded as **Apple Computer Company** (a partnership) and then as **Apple Computer, Inc.**
**Stage:** 1 of 3.
**File:** `company_004_apple/_parts/s1_p1.md` — **Header + window, STAGE BOUNDARY JUSTIFICATION, §A, §B, §C, §D.** Sections E–P and Q–U and the consolidated claim-record appendix are other volumes; section letters, record IDs and conflict numbering continue across parts (§9.3) and are never renumbered to make this part look self-contained. Cross-references take the form `(Apple S1 §D.1, s1_p1)`.
**Local record namespace:** this part mints **S1P1-01 … S1P1-nn** as dossier-local IDs (§13); global uniqueness lives only in `sources.csv` at merge, and no `source_id` is defined here.
**Window:** see §STAGE BOUNDARY JUSTIFICATION. The window is argued, not assumed; every candidate end-date in the record is tested there.
**Stage definition (working, defended below):** from the earliest document attested origin of the venture to the first meaningful real-world test of it — an artifact, a price, or an order that existed outside the founders' own assertion.
**Hindsight firewall (§2).** Nothing below treats Apple's later scale as evidence that a 1976–77 decision was rational, that any party was obviously right or wrong, or that any outcome was foreseeable. No 1980-or-later product, institution, price or reputation is imported into 1976–77. The anti-hagiography test applied to every section: *would this paragraph still read as plausible if the firm had failed in 1981?* Where it would not, the paragraph is rewritten or deleted. **Record-selection null (§2):** stated per section, because this is a survivor's archive — what does not survive is internal deliberation, rejected options, contemporaneous failure nobody printed, and any independent count behind a company self-report.
**Confidence (§3):** **High** = 2+ independent sources or a primary document; **Medium** = one reliable source, or approximate date corroborated later; **Low** = conflicting, vague, or retrospective-only; **UNKNOWN** = no evidence recovered. **UNKNOWN is a complete answer.**
**Independence and filing-lineage (§3).** Repeated reprinting of one origin story is ONE source. Documents inside one lineage — a corporate instrument and its later recital of itself, an advertisement and the company page that reprints it, a magazine's republication of another magazine's copy — are one source however many files they occupy. Corroboration requires an independent origin.
**Time audit (§6).** No statement imports later knowledge into an earlier period; a later source explaining an earlier event is tagged `RETROSPECTIVE SOURCE`. Numerals carry their basis: nominal 1976–77 dollars unless stated; units, boards, systems, dealers, dollars distinguished.
**Quotation discipline.** Quotation marks appear only around strings read at the cited line of a local file. Paraphrase is written unquoted and labelled. Line numbers are locators, not addresses (§14.12); each pointer carries a stable label alongside it.
**Zero web requests this pass.** Every unmet evidentiary need is recorded as UNTRIED with the exact query (§14.1, §14.4).


**MERGE NOTE — this volume (1 of 2) and its continuation (apple-s1-merge, 2026-09-26).** The three assembly parts measured 64,754 words between them, over the 60,000-word hard cap of §9.2, so §9.3 governs: the document is cut at one section boundary and continues in `stage_1_part_2.md`. **Volume 1 (`stage_1.md`) holds the Header, STAGE BOUNDARY JUSTIFICATION and §A–§U; volume 2 (`stage_1_part_2.md`) holds the consolidated `Untried routes` section and the carried-forward part handoff and pre-merge register-request blocks.** The boundary was chosen at the end of §U, not mid-table and mid-claim-record never, so the whole `U.nnn` anchor spine stays in one volume and the §7/§14 rule that numbering continues and is never renumbered is satisfied by construction. Cross-references keep the `(Apple S1 §D.1, s1_p1)` form and part pointers name the volume.

**What the merge did and did not do to this text.** Nothing inside any part was rewritten, trimmed or renumbered: the parts' words are here in their own order, with one document header, and each part's per-part `# s1_pN.md` line and its stale `SCAFFOLDED … nothing written yet` comment removed as scaffolding. §14 rule 4 and rule 12 forbid a silent tidy, so the corrections that `_parts/s1_p3.md` asked the merge to make **inside** `_parts/s1_p1.md` — the window labelling of records **S1P1-05** and **S1P1-06** (anchor **U.019**), the mis-pointed `Conflicts:` cell of record **S1P1-10** (anchor **U.011**), and the `Source`/`Tier`/`Conf` upgrades of **S1P1-01…11** from the resolving records — are **carried here as an instruction to the audit pass and are not applied to the released text**, because the released records are the evidence of what that pass wrote when it wrote it. Both anchors state the two-sided position on the record itself.

**Key governance (detail in `stage_1_index.md`).** Part-local and dossier-local keys have exactly one canonical home: `S1P1-CF-01…10` and `S1P2-CF-01…05` and the 13 `U-A2-*` register rows and the `U-AP-*`/`U-C-*` dossier keys are **aliases** whose canonical addresses are the `U.nnn` anchors of §U; `S1P3-S04` and `S1P2-S02` are one auction-lot lineage and were **not** given two ids, and the source ids `S1M-01…S1M-10` are assigned centrally at merge (§13) over the locally proposed `S1P2-S01…05`, `S1P3-S01…05` and the dossier-local `CS-01`/`CS-05`. Two evidentiary positions are unchanged by any of this: **`$666.66` is verified absent from every held byte and stays FOUNDER CLAIM or UNKNOWN**, and **Apple's earliest EDGAR text is the 1994-01-26 / FY1994 filing**, so everything earlier is print and one lineage is one source.

## STAGE BOUNDARY JUSTIFICATION

STATUS: WRITTEN 2026-09-25

**Both edges are argued below; neither is inherited.** The probe (`research/A_chronology_feasibility.md` §Recommended stage boundaries) recommends 1975 → 1977-01-03, and this part adopts that span after testing every candidate date the local corpus supplies. One of the probe's own supports had to be discarded first: its fact-list #4 and its start-edge confidence row both rest on the Homebrew newsletter of 1976-04-30, and `research/D_adversarial.md` L-3 establishes that the paragraph sits under the standing column heading "NOTES FROM THE NORTH", whose subject is **the Sonoma County Micro Computer Club** meeting in Cotati — so the document is a *reprinted club letter from a different club*, not a Homebrew record of Apple. This part therefore treats the machine's existence and naming in April 1976 as evidence (it is strong), and treats "Apple demoed at Homebrew in April 1976" as **unsupported and not used**.

**Window: 1975 (provisional, "late in 1975") → 1977-01-03.**

| Stage | Start | End | Why This Boundary | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 (this file) | 1975 ("late in 1975", per the designer's own 1977 print clause) | 1977-01-03 | Start = the first date the *product* is given by a first-party document, there being no dated 1975 artifact of the venture itself. End = the first **legal-entity** date in the record, on two documents of unrelated lineage. Between them the venture has no filing, no payroll, no charter and no maker-issued print at all: it exists only as a club letter, a dealer advertisement, an editorial clause and an unread partnership instrument. | Start: **Low–Medium** (one uncorroborated first-party clause committed to print two years later than the event it dates) · End: **High on the year, Medium on the day** |

### Start edge — candidates tested

| Candidate | What the record actually is | Adopted? |
|---|---|---|
| 1973 | Jobs's handwritten job application, photographed and sold at Charterfields, March 2021 for US$222,400 (`research/A_chronology_feasibility.md` AP-28) | **No.** Founder-state trace, not venture-state; and the date is the curator's inference ("believed to have been completed around"), confidence Low. |
| 1975-03-15 | Homebrew Computer Club Newsletter Vol 1 No 1, the start of the only non-company primary run for the period (AP-27) | **No.** It dates the milieu's documentation, not the venture. |
| 1975-09 | BYTE's founding issue prints **zero** occurrences of the company (B-15, via `research/B_founder_forensics.md` §Timeline) | **No** — used as the *silence baseline* against which 1976 is measured (§A, §D). |
| "late in 1975" | Wozniak, in his own byline: "The Apple-I, my first video oriented single board computer, was designed late in 1975" — `sources/ia_byte_1977/byte-1977-05.txt`, quoted at line level by `research/A2_periodical_archive_mine.md` A2-25 | **Adopted.** It is the only design date in the record and it is first-party. Class: **FOUNDER CLAIM (contemporaneous)** — spoken in May 1977, while the company was two months old and the outcome unknown — which is a better class than memoir but still a single uncorroborated origin: A2-25 records that "the *design date* is corroborated by **nothing** in this corpus". |

### End edge — every candidate in the record tested

| Candidate | Instrument / document | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| 1976-04-01 | Apple Computer Company partnership agreement, three typed pages, three signatures. Reaches this project only through Christie's lot 242 description and essay (`research/C_corporate_legal_org.md` C-27, C-29, C-34) and AP-20's secondary reports; **the instrument itself is unread** (D's L-8). | **Rejected as the end.** It is a formation paper, not a real-world test; and adopting it would close Stage 1 roughly three months after the adopted start, on a document this project has not read. It is used in §B as the strongest dated organization-claim available, priced accordingly. |
| 1976-04-30 | Sonoma County Micro Computer Club letter, reprinted in the Homebrew newsletter of that date: an "APPLE 6502 system" was the club's guest and "We are grateful to STEVE WOZNIAK for providing transportation" — re-read at the primary this pass (`sources/ia_homebrew/hcc0204.txt` l.140). | **Rejected as the end, adopted as the first dated artifact of the machine.** It is the earliest document anywhere in the corpus that names the product and names a founder in the same breath, outside the founders' own assertion. |
| 1976-09 | Advertisement of Computer Mart of New York, 314 Fifth Avenue: "Take a byte out of the new Apple-1 com-puter" — read at the primary this pass (`sources/ia_byte_1976/byte-1976-09.txt` l.25277). | **Rejected.** Earliest print naming the *product for sale*, and therefore the load-bearing validation rung inside the window (§D), but the venture's organisational transformation still lay ahead. |
| 1976-12-31 | The close of the year in which the company first appears in trade print. | **Rejected as arbitrary.** A calendar boundary chosen for tidiness; the record supplies legal events that outrank it. |
| **1977-01-03** | "Apple Computer, Inc. ("Apple" or the "Company") was incorporated under the laws of the State of California on January 3, 1977." — **read at the primary this pass**, `sources/10-K_FY1994_acc-0000320193-94-000016_filed-1994-12-13.txt` l.132–133, where the sentence is hard-wrapped and does not match a whole-string grep (D's L-7.1). Independent witness: "Apple, incorporated in 1977, reported profits of $11.7 million on sales of $117 million for the fiscal year ending September 26, 1980." — **read at the primary this pass**, `sources/ia_byte_1981/byte-1981-02.txt` l.48497–48498, also hard-wrapped. | **ADOPTED.** Two Tier-1 documents of **unrelated lineage** — a registrant filing and an unsigned trade-press column, different author, publisher and decade — agree on the year (B's H-1). The day rests on the filing alone, so the day is Medium and the year High. |
| 1977-04 / 1977-06 / 1977-07 | The Apple II's billed introduction at the first West Coast Computer Faire; BYTE's April 1977 national store directory (A2-35); Apple's first self-published advertisement with its nine-row price ladder and mail-order form (A2-27, A2-28, A2-29; B-35). | **Rejected — and the cost of rejecting it is stated.** These are the two or three best contemporaneous *commercial* artifacts the corpus holds, and they sit **after** the adopted boundary. They are used in this file only as post-window witnesses to the channel the window's product entered, always labelled by their date, never as Stage-1 state. See §Boundary cost, immediately below. |

**Boundary cost and how it is handled.** Because the window closes at 1977-01-03, every artifact that prints a maker's price, an address of record, a telephone, a payment instrument or a dealer list post-dates it: "Inc." first appears in Apple's own printed voice in June 1977 (C-05), the corporation's advertising capacity is visible only from June–September 1977 (A2-30, AP-22's superseding marker), and the price ladder is June 1977 (A2-28). Two claim records minted by the interrupted pass describe the April 1977 directory and the June/July 1977 order form as "inside the window" (S1P1-05, S1P1-06). Under the adopted boundary they are **outside** it. This part does not silently re-date them; the defect is registered as **S1P1-CF-10** and the records are corrected there rather than deleted here.

**Two dated events, never one "founding" (D's L-8 merge decision).** The 1976-04-01 instrument and the 1977-01-03 incorporation are different events of different legal character, nine months apart, and **no document in this corpus makes one a continuation of the other** (C-01). The word "founded" is therefore not used in this file without attaching one of the two dates to it. What the succession mechanics were — conversion, new charter over the old firm, or something else — is UNKNOWN: the California Secretary of State entity file, which the company's own 1994 filing names as the charter registry (C-01's AP-06 route, and the exhibit index line "88-S3 Restated Articles of Incorporation, filed with the Secretary of State of the State of California on January 27, 1988"), was tried and not answered (C-07).

**Place of formation is not Los Altos.** The universe register's cell reads "1976-04-01, Los Altos, California" and traces there to a Wikipedia raw page (A's header). Against it: the lot's own bibliographic line places the signing "[Mountain View, California]" (C-34); 1976 dated print places the firm nowhere at all; the first address of record is Cupertino, 20863 Stevens Creek Blvd Bldg B3-C, and it is printed May–June **1977** (A2-25, C-05); and "garage" attaches to Apple in this corpus **only** in the February 1981 gloss "who took Apple from a garage operation to its current enviable position" (C-11, read at `sources/ia_byte_1981/byte-1981-02.txt` per L-5). Worse for the motif's evidentiary weight, the same magazine printed the garage as a **stock figure of the field** about another firm in July 1976 — "all the company consisted of was two people in a garage" of Sphere (L-5, `sources/ia_byte_1976/byte-1976-07.txt` l.3023–3027, under the issue-date caveat below). Premises: **UNKNOWN** (D's DG-D5).

**Provenance risk carried on the boundary itself (L-6).** The 1976 BYTE issues are dated by Internet Archive metadata plus internal cross-references that **hold for October–December and fail for July**: the `-07` file previews pieces appearing in "BYTE's August and September issues" yet narrates events through "October 30". Nothing in the adopted boundary depends solely on `-07` — the start edge rests on BYTE May 1977 and the end edge on the 1994 filing and BYTE February 1981 — but every Stage-1 statement sourced to a Jan–Sep 1976 BYTE file carries `issue-date: provisional pending page-sequence verification`, and month confidence for those files is **Medium**, not High.

**Record-selection null for this section (§2).** The boundary is drawn where paper survives, not where decisions were taken. There is no 1976 filing of any kind, no charter text, no partnership clause this project has read, and no rejected alternative — no document records anyone *considering* a later date, staying a partnership, or dissolving. A boundary built from the first surviving legal instrument is therefore a boundary set by the **archive's shape**; a firm that had filed papers in 1975 would have a different Stage 1, and nothing in the record shows the chosen edge was the one the participants experienced.

## A. EXECUTIVE STATE SUMMARY

STATUS: WRITTEN 2026-09-25

**What the venture was at 1977-01-03, as far as dated public evidence reaches.** It was a machine that existed, was named by a third party, and was being sold **by other people's advertisements before it had any commercial voice of its own**. Between the Sonoma County club letter of 1976-04-30 and the end of the window, the company appears in the surviving record as: one dealer's advertisement naming "the new Apple-1 computer" (New York, September 1976, re-run October, November, December), one mail-order dealer's price sheet offering it at "10% discount from manufacturer's current list prices" (Berkeley, November 1976), one technical article listing "the new Apple computer" among machines "currently being marketed" (November 1976), one editorial clause naming "Steven Jobs (Apple Computer Co)" (December 1976), one exhibitor list naming "Apple Computers" (December 1976, and the club's own list of 1976-09-15), and a partnership instrument nobody in this project has read. **The maker placed no advertisement of its own in any 1976 document in this corpus** — AP-22, independently re-confirmed by a second exhaustive pass at A2-18/A2-65 — and printed no price, no address, no telephone and no letterhead.

| Variable | Value | Source | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| Dated legal events inside the window | 1976-04-01 partnership instrument; 1976-04-12 one-page amendment letter signed by all three; 1977-01-03 incorporation (California) | `research/C_corporate_legal_org.md` C-27, C-28, C-01; `sources/10-K_FY1994_acc-0000320193-94-000016_filed-1994-12-13.txt` l.132–133 | High (the incorporation); Medium (the two 1976 papers, from a Tier-3 catalogue of an unread object) |
| Legal form at window end | Corporation from 1977-01-03; before that, a trading partnership with **no filed formation record located** | C-01, C-07 | High / the succession mechanics UNKNOWN |
| Entity name as actually printed in 1976 | "Apple Computer Co"; "Apple Computers"; "Apple Computer Inc." appears **in no 1976 document**; "Inc." enters only in Apple's own printed voice, June 1977 | C-04, C-05 | High |
| Founders named in in-window print | Jobs: one clause (BYTE December 1976). Wozniak: one reprinted club letter (1976-04-30) and the 1976-11-20 sighting column published April 1977. Wayne: **zero**. Markkula: **zero** | A2-61a; C-21; `research/B_founder_forensics.md` §B.3, §B.4 | High |
| Officer title or governance office for any principal | **None documented in the window or anywhere in the cached corpus.** "No occurrence of 'president', 'vice president', 'chairman', 'treasurer', 'secretary', 'board of directors' or 'officer' anywhere in the corpus is joined to an Apple name" | C-13 | High for this corpus, Medium as a general statement (BYTE Aug 1977–Nov 1980 is not cached) |
| First product | A 6502 single-board video computer, sold "as a completely assembled and tested processor board" | A2-25, A2-26; A2-20 (6502 as a contemporaneous 1976 fact) | High (form as stated); High (processor) |
| Price of the first product, in-window | **UNKNOWN.** No 1976 document states it; the maker's only printed figure is the designer's rounded band "a price under $700 at the retail level", published May 1977 | A2-23 (documented absence), A2-26 | High that it is absent here; Medium for the band as a band |
| The famous hobby-kit price | **UNKNOWN as a figure.** $666.66 occurs in **no in-window document**; the corpus's only dollar-shaped 666 is a competitor's strike-through anchor ("IMSAI 8080 … a $666 value / only $595"). Its carriers are a 2021 merchant conversation and the 2026 auction essay | D's L-2 table; U-A2-4 | High on absence in these files / UNKNOWN on the true figure |
| Units produced or sold | **UNKNOWN.** "About 200 Apple-1s" is a wiki-derived count whose only cited source is a 2011 Wikipedia retrieval (D's L-9); no in-window document counts units | D's L-9; A2-23 | High that it is undocumented |
| First customer / first order | **UNKNOWN.** "50 boards at $500, July 1976" traces to one merchant's later conversations, contradicts itself on date and price, and is re-printed without a document by the commercial custodian of the founding paper | D's L-1; U-C-4 | Low; the *relationship* is documented, the transaction is not |
| Dealers carrying the product, in-window | Two named retailers, of **one origin each**: Computer Mart of New York (September, four 1976 printings = one campaign) and Kentucky Fried Computers, Berkeley (November). **No 1976 document evidences an authorized Apple dealer relationship of any kind** | D's L-4; A2-08, A2-11, A2-12, A2-38 | High (the advertisements exist as printed) |
| Revenue / profit FY1976, FY1977 | **UNKNOWN.** No in-window document establishes either. The printed series begins at FY1978 ($7.8 million sales, $793,497 profit), in an unsigned column dated February 1981 | AP-35; A2-49; D's L-11 | High that it is absent; the FY1978–80 series rests on **one** witness |
| Capital contributed by any founder | **UNKNOWN.** $1,000 (HP-65 + VW bus) is a 2006 autobiography with 0 independent corroboration; the $15,000 loan is one participant's memory; Wayne's $800 (plus a reported later $1,500) reaches the record through auction coverage | C-10; AP-29, AP-32 | Folklore class; Low |
| Outside money inside the window | **UNKNOWN.** The only printed trace of venture capital is "Venrock Associates, a venture capital firm, holds 3.8 million shares" — February 1981, a holding, not a round, and no date | C-12; AP-18 | Medium as printed / UNKNOWN as Stage-1 event |
| Premises | **UNKNOWN** as to any 1976 address. First address of record: 20863 Stevens Creek Blvd Bldg B3-C, Cupertino, printed May–June 1977 | A2-25, C-05, D's DG-D5 | High (the 1977 address) / UNKNOWN (1976 premises) |
| People other than the founders associated with the product | Four names, credited for software and I/O labour, in a May 1977 acknowledgement box: Allen Baum, Doug Kraul, Randy Wigginton, Chris Espinosa. **No title, wage, hire date or headcount appears anywhere**; employee-numbering claims are not in these files | A2-33 | High (as printed) / headcount UNKNOWN |
| The corporation's own first month in the record | **Empty.** The club newsletters of 1976-12-10 and 1977-01-19 carry zero body occurrences of the name, after five issues carrying it across 1976; BYTE's 1977 Apple coverage does not resume until April | C-20 | High that the two issues are silent — **not** that the firm was inactive |

**The finding, stated as the record supports it.** Stage 1's substance is not a company being built; it is a **product entering a market through other people's paper**. The single most load-bearing asymmetry in the Apple corpus is that the venture's first nine printed months contain *no maker-generated commercial document whatsoever* — no price, no offer, no address — while they contain two independent retailers' claims to be selling the thing (AP-22 with its superseding marker; A2-65, which draws exactly this conclusion). What follows without hindsight is narrow but firm: a partnership formed around a machine had, by September 1976, produced something a New York hobby shop thought worth advertising, and by December 1976 an editor who dealt in four other companies' hardware treated one of its principals as a peer. A2-65 names the mechanism question the corpus cannot answer, and this file does not answer it either: **why the maker did not advertise in 1976 is UNKNOWN** — money, capacity, or having no literature to print are the three available explanations and none is evidenced.

**What the window-end legal event does *not* buy.** The 1977-01-03 incorporation is the most certain date in this stage and, by itself, tells nothing about capital, control, officers, or purpose: no charter text is held (C-07), no incorporation-era stock record surfaced, and the month's own print is silent (C-20). The temptation this file must resist is the one the record-selection null exists to name — reading a legal milestone as an organisational one.

**Hindsight-firewall test applied to this section (§2).** Every row above is a dated document's statement. Nothing here treats the choice to incorporate, the choice to sell through dealers, or the choice of the 6502 as vindicated by anything after 1980. A reader who knew only this section would know the firm's revenue, units, prices, first customer, premises, officers and capital as UNKNOWN — which is, on this evidence, the accurate description of Apple at 1977-01-03.

**Record-selection null (§2).** What is unrecoverable about this period is unrecoverable because the surviving archive is not the company's. The record-keeping parties are an editor (BYTE), a club (its newsletter run) and dealers (their advertisements). They preserved products, prices, stock lists and their own gossip about the field. They preserved **no** internal deliberation, **no** rejected option, **no** contemporaneous failure, and **no** independent count behind any company self-report. Concretely: the founders' interior — what they were paid, what they owned, who refused them, what they argued about, what they could raise — leaves no trace in this family at all (`research/B_founder_forensics.md` §header, citing A2-61a: `Wozniak` in four cached files, `Jobs` as a personal name in three, `Markkula` once in ~13 MB, `Wayne` never). Every one of the UNKNOWNs in the table above is a property of that selection, not a finding that the corresponding fact was small.

## B. FOUNDER / COMPANY STATE

STATUS: WRITTEN 2026-09-25

**Governing rule for this section, and why it reads thin.** A field is populated only where a dated document populates it. The Apple corpus is a **trade** archive, not a personnel one: `research/A2_periodical_archive_mine.md` A2-61a's name census over ~13 MB and 30 cached files finds `Wozniak` in **four** files, `Jobs` as a personal name in **three**, `Markkula` **once**, and Ronald Wayne **never** (C-21: 23 body hits for `Wayne`, all unrelated — Fort Wayne, Wayne State, Wayne Green, Wayne Sewell). A2-61b sharpens it: neither founder's name appears in any BYTE advertising page of October, November or December 1976, while the brand "Apple" appears in dealers' pages with no person attached. The mechanism is named there rather than guessed: "in 1976 the hobby trade dealt in **brands on dealer pages**, not in founders". Everything said about "Jobs in 1976" or "Wozniak in 1976" is therefore imported from a source class that was not watching them.

### B.1 Stephen (Steve) Jobs

| Field | Value in the window | Document | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| Named in print | Once, four words: "Steven Jobs (Apple Computer Co)", inside Carl Helmers' list of "entrepeneurs" he spoke with "on the floor of the WESCON show last September in Los Angeles CA" | BYTE December 1976, `sources/ia_byte_1976/byte-1976-12.txt` (A2-15, A2-56, B-31; AP-10) | High that it was printed; Medium as a standing claim |
| Age | UNKNOWN in-window. Sole witness is post-window: "Steve Jobs, 25 years old" (BYTE February 1981) → **DERIVED** birth c. Feb 1955, hence c. 20–22 at the 1976-04-01 partnership, integer ages ±1 yr | `sources/ia_byte_1981/byte-1981-02.txt` (A2-60; `research/B_founder_forensics.md` §B.1) | Medium for the printed age; Low for the 1976 inference |
| Prior employer | **UNKNOWN at Tier 1, and outside this evidence family entirely.** `Atari` occurs in the cached 1975–77 files only as the trademark notice "PONG is a trademark of Atari Inc." inside Apple's own advertisement, and `Hewlett`/`Packard`/`HP-65` only in calculator and component contexts (A2-61c). No payroll, directory or personnel document in this corpus connects him to any employer | A2-61c; B-38, B-42 | High (documented absence in family); **Low on the employment claim itself**, which rests on curated auction prose |
| Technical work product attributed to him | **None in-window.** No 1976–77 document attributes a design, circuit, program, manual or article to Jobs (A2-59: the print "does not say"). One qualification B-32 supplies: an outside witness describes a programming session he took part in — which bars both "he wrote the software" and "no technical act of his was ever witnessed" | A2-59, B-32 | High (absence of attribution) / Low (the session's meaning) |
| Commercial function attributed | Not stated in any 1976–77 text (A2-59, B-08). The one functional designation available is the **unread 1976 instrument as read by a 2026 catalogue**, which lists Jobs's responsibility as "Electrical Engineering and Marketing" | C-29 (Tier-3 catalogue quoting a Tier-1 document this project has not read) | Medium, correctly vintaged |
| Documented locations of presence | WESCON floor, Los Angeles, September 1976; a Palo Alto motel room, 1976-11-20, with Wozniak and a prototype. Residence: **UNKNOWN**; 20211 Crist Drive, Los Altos is UNSOURCED within the period and not refuted | A2-31, B-31, E-07, H-7 | Medium-High for presence |
| What he could put up | **UNKNOWN.** $500 for an HP-65 and $750 for a VW bus is a 2006 autobiography with **0 independent corroboration** (AP-32); the $15,000 loan is one participant's retrospective memory (AP-29). D's C-9 marker adds only a *plausibility bound* from in-window print — BYTE April 1976 on that calculator: "the HP-65 retails for $795 (although it can sometimes be obtained for $695 if one looks hard enough)" — and expressly forbids citing that bound as a witness to the event | AP-32 + its superseding marker; C-10 | Folklore / Low |
| Officer title | **None** in any 1976–77 document, and none anywhere in the cached corpus (C-13) | C-13, B-37 | High (absence) |

### B.2 Stephen (Steve) Wozniak

| Field | Value in the window | Document | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| First dated appearance of his name | 1976-04-30, in a **different club's** letter reprinted by Homebrew: "In April the APPLE 6502 system was our special guest. We are grateful to STEVE WOZNIAK for providing transportation." Re-read at the primary this pass, `sources/ia_homebrew/hcc0204.txt` l.140 | A2-19; corrected provenance per D's L-3 | Medium-High (that the letter says this); High that the machine existed and was named by April 1976 |
| Age | UNKNOWN in-window; "Steve Wozniak, 30 years old", February 1981 → **DERIVED** c. 25–26 at the partnership date, ±1 yr | A2-60 | Medium / Low |
| Design credit, by an outside party | "designer of the Apple-ll computer" — BYTE's own editor, April 1977. Post-window, and the earliest design attribution in the corpus by anyone | A2-32, B-33 | High (as printed) |
| Work product in evidence | A 6502 single-board video computer whose existence, name and processor are all third-party-dated in 1976 (A2-20, A2-19); the machine demonstrated to an editor in person on 1976-11-20 with a 5K BASIC interpreter, in which the editor and both founders wrote a game "After perhaps 30 to 45 minutes" | A2-31 | High |
| Technical standing | BYTE attributes the interpreter to him in the possessive and indexes him as the author of its May 1977 system description; a club contributor separately reports using "the Apple Computer" for a television-monitor modification — the only third party shown **operating** his machine | B-34, B-41, A2-22 | Medium-High |
| Unpaid mobility as evidence of means | The April 1976 letter documents him driving a machine roughly 60 miles to a club outside his own county: self-mobile distribution of his own design, not a venture. His own printed account of how the product moved names **no customer, no order, no unit count and no price figure** (E-11/E-12, L-10) | A2-19; A2-25, A2-26 | Medium / High (the documented blind spot) |
| Address of record | Cupertino, 20863 Stevens Creek Blvd B3-C — first printed **May 1977**, in his own byline. Post-window; no 1976 document gives any address for any person | A2-25, C-05 | High (as printed) / UNKNOWN for 1976 |
| Officer title | **None** (C-13). The unread instrument's catalogue reading gives him "Electrical Engineering" | C-29 | Medium, vintaged |

### B.3 Ronald Wayne — absent from the window, and what that bars

**Vintage warning, stated before any claim.** Wayne appears in **zero** in-window documents. Nothing in this sub-section may be cited as a 1976 record about him; every line is either a claim about a document this project has not read or a 2018–2026 retrospective witness (B §B.3).

| Field | Value | Document | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| Presence in the founding-era record | **None.** His name is absent from all 16 cached BYTE issues and all 13 newsletters (C-21; A2-52, A2-73). A third signatory is invisible to the entire trade press of his own company's founding year — a statement about the record, not about him | C-21, A2-61a | High (as a record statement) |
| Existence as a signatory | Three named signatures on a dated 1976 instrument: "Typed document signed ('Stephen G. Wozniak', 'steven p jobs' and 'Ronald Wayne'), [Mountain View, California] 1 April 1976. Three pages, letter-sized paper." Plus a one-page "Amendment letter to the above", same three signatures, 12 April 1976 | C-27, C-28 — **a Tier-3 auction catalogue describing a Tier-1 object; the clauses are not transcribed and this project has not read either paper** | High (existence, dates, signatories as catalogued); **UNKNOWN (every clause)** |
| Stake | **CONFLICT — both sides recorded, see S1P1-CF-07.** Catalogue side: "Wayne received a 10% stake", consistent with a 45/45/10 allocation, which exhausts 100 (C-34 area, §Contradictions of `research/C_corporate_legal_org.md`; AP-20; U-C-4). Canon side: 12%, circulating in 2024–26 retellings (AP-20, and A's Famous-claims table). 12% cannot coexist with 45/45/10 | Catalogue of an unread instrument vs un-sourced retelling | **Medium-High on 10%; High that 12% is unsupported by any document; UNKNOWN on which the instrument says** — because the instrument is unread |
| Duration and consideration | **CONFLICT.** 1 April → 12 April is **eleven days**, and the interval is internally corroborated by the lot's own two dates — the strongest single documentary point in the whole Apple corpus (C-28). The ubiquitous "12 days" is a rounding. Consideration: catalogue "$800"; AP-20's reporting adds "a later $1,500". Motivation ("wary of the financial risk") is the cataloguer's retrospective sentence, not a clause | C-28; AP-20 | High (the dated amendment) / Low (money and motive) |
| Function | "Mechanical Engineering and Documentation", per the catalogue's reading of the instrument (C-29) | Tier-3 quoting Tier-1 | Medium |
| How he entered the story | "a 41-year-old product designer he had met at Atari", who "had prior experience founding a company", and who "drafted this original partnership agreement (apparently cobbled together using prior examples to save the expense of a lawyer)" | C-30, Christie's lot essay, 2026-01-23 — **0 corroboration, and the cataloguer's own hedge "apparently" is in the sentence** | Low; RETROSPECTIVE INTERPRETATION. Note the double interest: this is simultaneously the only carrier connecting the founding to Atari and a passage written to sell the lot |
| Age, education, residence, network | UNKNOWN at every date, from any vintage | — | UNKNOWN |

### B.4 A. C. (Mike) Markkula — the terms are a live conflict, not a settled fact

**Vintage warning.** His entire footprint in ~13 MB of in-window trade print is **one sentence in one unsigned column, BYTE February 1981**: "A C Markkula, 32 years old, who took Apple from a garage operation to its current enviable position, also holds 8.3 million shares" (C-11, A2-51). He occurs **zero times in every cached file dated 1975 through December 1980** (A's superseding marker on the Markkula row). Any statement about his 1976 status must come from a later memoir; this file declines to make one.

| Field | Value | Document | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| Status in-window | **UNKNOWN.** No document in the corpus says when he arrived, what he was offered, or what he agreed to. His name enters the record only once the company is already public | C-11, A2-51, DG-5 | High (that the record is empty) |
| Holding | 8.3 million shares — the same count printed for Jobs and for Wozniak, in February 1981. A holding, not a transaction, and undated as to when it was acquired | AP-18, A2-51 | Medium-High as printed / UNKNOWN as to origin |
| Terms of entry | **CONFLICT — record both sides, see S1P1-CF-06.** Canon side: a $91,000 bank guarantee, roughly $250,000 of investment, and a 26–28% stake (post-1981 book retellings). Corpus side: **these figures appear in no retrieved document at all** — C-10 states it as the dossier's central negative, and A's Famous-claims row records that only his existence, role and holding survive the test | C-10; AP-18; B §B.4 | Terms: **UNKNOWN at Tier 1, folklore class**. Best-supported: none available |
| Role | "third co-founder" — asserted by the 2026 auction essay (C-33). "Took Apple from a garage operation to its current enviable position" — asserted in 1981 by a writer who did not document the transition. Neither is a document; C-33's own corroboration cell declines to count the essay as a third witness to the incorporation | C-33, C-11, L-5 | Low as Stage-1 evidence / High that the sentences were printed |
| Age | c. 27–28 at the partnership date, **DERIVED** backwards from "32 years old" in February 1981, ±1 yr | A2-60 | Low |
| Network edge to either founder | **No document at any date in the corpus.** The first print of him does not describe an introduction | B §Network map, final rows | UNKNOWN |

### B.5 Company state: the name, the entity, and the absence of offices

| Variable | Value | Source | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| Name forms in authentic use, 1976–77 | Four, none of them the partnership's registered style: "Apple Computer Co" (BYTE Dec 1976; still Wozniak's own byline in May 1977), "Apple Computers" (BYTE Dec 1976 exhibitor list; Homebrew 1976-09-15), "Apple Computer Inc." (Apple's own advertisement, June 1977), "the Apple Computer booth" (BYTE April 1977) | C-04, C-05, A2-32, U-A2-1 | High |
| A corporation evidenced by any 1976 document | **None.** 1976 usage is a trading style | C-04 | High |
| Corporate-styled self-identification, first instance | June 1977: "Mail to: Apple Computer Inc., 20863 Stevens Creek Blvd., B3-C, Cupertino, California 95014" — five months after the incorporation date, and C-05's own caution applies: "an advertisement can say 'Inc.' whether or not a charter exists" | C-05 | High (printed) / it is a marker, not proof |
| Name-form drift after incorporation | The partnership-era style kept legal life for years: a third party's dated citation in BYTE February 1981 renders a December 1979 Apple publication as "Cupertino CA: Apple Computer Co, Dec 1979", while advertisers in the same issue write "Apple Computers Inc." and "the Apple Corporation" | C-06 | High (as printed). Consequence: trade-name usage is **weak** evidence of corporate identity in this period |
| Governance offices | **Not establishable in the Stage-1 record — and not establishable anywhere in the cached corpus** (C-13). Absence of a title in the record is **not** proof none existed: the corpus skips BYTE August 1977–November 1980 entirely, and trade press of this class rarely printed officer lists. The correct statement is "the earliest documented officer of Apple Computer, Inc. is not establishable from any retrieved document"; the California SoS statement of information or the 1980 prospectus is the route that could settle it | C-13 | High for this corpus / Medium as a general statement |
| Division of labour | **Asymmetric in the record and the asymmetry is the finding:** the only labour split visible in 1976–77 print is inside one man — Wozniak as designer-cum-programmer — plus a co-presence whose function is never stated (Jobs). The one exception, correctly vintaged, is the unread instrument's three-way function allocation as quoted by its 2026 cataloguer (C-29), which qualifies C-13 in both directions: no office is named, yet the paper recorded duties | A2-59, B-32, C-29 | High (the print asymmetry) / Medium (the instrument, via catalogue) |
| The documented network, read as a whole | Editor, clubs, dealers, a neighbouring firm (Shepardson Microsystems, three building numbers away at 20823 Stevens Creek Blvd), and the Faire organisers. **The three edges a Stage-1 founder chapter most needs — Jobs→Terrell, either founder→Markkula, either founder→Wayne — are the ones the record does not supply**; two are unknown at any date and one exists only as names on the same printed page | B §Network map; A2-58, B-40 | Medium-High as to edges present; UNKNOWN as to introductions |

**Anti-hagiography test on this section.** Nothing above asserts that any of the four was gifted, far-sighted, or the right person for a function. Jobs is documented as a name in a clause and a man in a motel room; Wozniak as a designer whose own account omits every commercial particular; Wayne as a signatory invisible to his own company's trade press; Markkula as a share count and an opinion printed five years on. Had the firm failed in 1981, every sentence here would still read the same way — which is the test §2 asks for.

**Record-selection null, §B.** The founders' interior leaves no trace in this evidence family: no payroll, no diary, no correspondence, no meeting minute, no rejected option, no failure anybody printed. What survives of the founding paper is **one four-page object in a private owner's hands, sold twice at auction, whose clauses this project has never read** (C-27 to C-31, and D's L-8 "readiness attack"). A Stage-1 founder chapter built on this record can only be a chapter of **dated utterances about dated utterances**, and the file says so where it means it.

## C. ORIGINAL PROBLEM

STATUS: WRITTEN 2026-09-25

For a hardware venture of this period the standard §7 frame — a problem statement someone wrote down and a customer someone named — does not apply unmodified. **No document in this corpus contains a problem statement, a mission, a market study, or a named customer need belonging to the partnership.** What the record supplies instead is a three-part assembly: (i) the designer's own contemporaneous account of what the first machine was *intended* to be, (ii) a countable population whose equipment the machine addressed, and (iii) a category the trade press was coining at the same moment, in retail terms. Those three are what "the original problem" is establishable as, and the section is written to that limit.

### C.1 What the designer says the first product was for

| Variable | Value | Source | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| Stated original intent | "The Apple-I video computer board was originally intended as a television terminal product which could also operate in a stand alone mode without much in the way of memory, although it did have a processor, space for 8 K bytes of 4 K dynamic memory chips, and its shared video generation and dynamic memory refresh logic." | Wozniak, BYTE May 1977, quoted at line level by A2-26 (`sources/ia_byte_1977/byte-1977-05.txt`) — FOUNDER CLAIM (**contemporaneous**) | High (as printed) / Medium (as intent, being one first-party voice) |
| What that intent is *not* | It is not a personal-computer ambition. A2-26's own note states the consequence: the first product "was conceived as a *peripheral substitute*, not as a computer — the opposite of the later story about intending a personal computer" | A2-26 | High that the sentence says this |
| Stated novelty | "I think that the Apple-I computer was the first microprocessor system product on the market to completely integrate the display generation circuitry, microprocessor, memory and power supply on the same board." | A2-25 | Medium — the hedge "I think" is in the quotation, and **no competitor's board specification in this corpus was tested against it** |
| Delivered form | "Apple-I was sold as a completely assembled and tested processor board with a price under $700 at the retail level." | A2-26 | High (as printed) / the price is a **band, not a schedule** |
| Processor | 6502 — a contemporaneous 1976 fact, not a 1977 recollection | A2-20, A2-19 | High |
| What the buyer still had to supply | Nothing in any in-window document says. The only printed accessories statement is post-window and concerns the successor: the Apple II buyer still had to buy a television and "an inexpensive modulator (not supplied)" | A2-27 (June 1977) | UNKNOWN for the Apple I |

**Why this matters and what it does not license.** The single dated first-party statement of intent describes a **television-terminal** problem: replacing an expensive teletype or terminal with the receiver already in the room, and adding a processor and a little memory so the thing could run by itself. That is a cost-substitution problem inside an existing hobby, not the creation of a demand category. The firewall discipline here is to refuse both directions: it does not follow that "the personal-computer vision" is false, and it does not follow that the founder's 1977 description of 1975 intent is a reliable record of 1975 intent — a statement made while the company was two months old, about a design made while it did not exist, is contemporaneous **as to its making** and retrospective **as to its subject**. Recorded as conflict **S1P1-CF-11** rather than resolved.

### C.2 The population the machine addressed, as it was countable in period

The honest version of "the market" for Stage 1 is not a market. It is a **surveyed room**, and the survey is in the corpus.

| Variable | Value | Source | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| Club equipment census, 1976-06-09 | "A survey of hobbyists attending the June 9th meeting reveals the following distributions of CPUs in use: 8080-53, 6502-18, 8008-6, PDP-8-4, LSI-11-3, Z80-2, 4004-1, PDP1 1/20-1 and TTL-1. That totals 101 systems up and running out the the group. About 250 hobbyists were at this meeting." [sic] | Robert Reiling, "Random Data", Homebrew Newsletter Vol 2 No 6, `sources/ia_homebrew/hcc0206.txt` — A2-61 | Medium-High. **An organiser's self-report; there is no independent denominator** |
| Same body, eight months earlier | "A similar survey in the October 15, 1975 meeting turned up 38 systems with about 80 hobbyists attending that meeting." | A2-61 | Medium-High, same caveat |
| Growth, **DERIVED** | systems `101 / 38 = 2.66×`; attendance `250 / 80 = 3.13×`, over roughly eight months | A2-61's arithmetic, restated | Arithmetic is certain; the base counts are one self-report |
| Share of the installed base the design targeted, **DERIVED** | the chosen 6502 sat at `18 / 101 = 17.8%` of live club systems, second to the 8080 at `53 / 101 = 52.5%` | A2-61 | Medium-High |
| Ceiling of the countable audience | Several hundred people in one Bay Area club, in one month, in one survey. The field's own ambition line for the retail channel is the same order of magnitude: a 1977 columnist contemplating a franchise plan that would "double or quadruple the present number of computer stores in existence" — i.e. a store population small enough to count | A2-61; A2-66 (`sources/ia_byte_1977/byte-1977-04.txt`) | High that it was printed |

**Reading, with the hindsight guard on.** This is not evidence that a mass market existed or that anyone saw one. It is evidence that the audience a Stage-1 Apple could reach was **countable, self-surveyed, and several hundred people wide**, and that the processor the design chose was already the second-most-owned chip in that room. The problem, stated in the terms the period makes available, is a fit problem inside a known population — not an invention of demand.

### C.3 The category the field was coining while the machine was being sold

| Variable | Value | Source | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| The in-period definition of the problem in retail terms | "An 'appliance computer' is by definition a completed system which is purchased off the retail shelf, taken home, plugged in and used." | Carl Helmers, BYTE April 1977 — A2-31 (`sources/ia_byte_1977/byte-1977-04.txt`) | High (as printed). Post-window by three months; used as the field's category statement, not as Stage-1 state |
| Its application to Apple, as printed | "The Apple-ll, which is to be introduced in April at the first West Coast Computer Faire in San Francisco, **may be** the first product to fully qualify as the 'appliance computer'" | AP-15 / A2-31 | High. The hedge is in the sentence; **it must not be quoted as a prophecy** |
| The assembly burden the category answers | The same pages sell competing machines in **two** prices, kit and assembled, so the cost of putting a computer in a case was a published line item: Processor Technology's Sol-20 at "$995, in kit form" and "$1495 fully assembled and tested … a beautiful case with solid walnut sides" | A2-45 (`sources/ia_byte_1976/byte-1976-11.txt`, `ia_byte_1977/byte-1977-04.txt`) | High |
| The maker's own assembly premium, **DERIVED** | Apple's 1977 premium for assembled over board-only: `1298 − 598 = $700`. Processor Technology's: `1495 − 995 = $500`. Ratio `700 / 500 = 1.40` | A2-28's ladder and A2-45, arithmetic as recorded there | High (arithmetic on printed rows). A2-45's caution governs: "it says nothing about which was the better decision" |
| The printed price geography the first machine sat in | Wozniak's "under $700 … at the retail level" for an assembled video board was **above** a kit mother-board price ($619.95 IMSAI 8080) and **below** the $849.95 Z-80-board and $1,099.95 assembled-terminal rows on the same dealer pages; the only 1976-printed complete 6502 system is Electronic Tool Co's at **$675**, "fully assembled, tested and ready to run" | A2-46, A2-26 with D's C-9 marker (`sources/ia_homebrew/hcc0206.txt` l.365–375) | High (rows as printed). The comparison is not like-for-like — the $675 is a *system*, the Apple figure a *board* — which makes the printed comparator **less** generous to Apple, not more |
| Consequence for reading the 1977 jump | The move from a sub-$700 board to a $1,298 assembled system is, on the printed rows alone, a **change of product category**, not a price rise | A2-46's inference, adopted | Medium-High, flagged as inference from printed rows |

### C.4 Knowability, per §7

| Item | Status |
|---|---|
| That a video-equipped 6502 board existed, was named "Apple", and was described by its designer as integrated on one board | **FACT / KNOWABLE** — April 1976 club letter (existence, naming, third-party), May 1977 designer's text (integration claim) |
| That the first product was framed as a television-terminal substitute rather than a personal computer | **KNOWABLE, one first-party voice** — FOUNDER CLAIM (contemporaneous), uncorroborated |
| The size and composition of the audience actually addressable in 1976 | **KNOWABLE as a self-reported count** — 101 systems, ~250 attendees, 6502 second-most-owned; no independent denominator |
| The retail problem the field was articulating (kit burden, cabinet, "plug in and use") | **KNOWABLE** — printed kit/assembled price pairs and Helmers' definition |
| Whether a paying customer outside the founders' own circle asked for this product, and what they asked for | **NOT KNOWABLE in this corpus** — no document in the record states a customer requirement, a request, or a complaint about the Apple I |
| Whether the machine was designed to be sold at all, or sold because it could be | **UNKNOWN.** The record cannot separate the two: the intent sentence describes a product, and the first dated commercial act is *another firm advertising it* (§D). Naming the mechanism here would be invention |
| What options were considered and rejected in 1975–76 | **NOT KNOWABLE.** No internal document survives in any family searched; see the null below |

**Record-selection null (§2), §C.** Everything in this section is recovered from a **survivor's** print: a magazine that carried the product, a club that counted its own members, and one designer who wrote an article. Unrecoverable because it was never kept: any rejected alternative (8080 instead of 6502; terminal instead of board; kit instead of assembled), any contemporaneous failure of the Apple I — returns, defects, unsold stock, an abandoned configuration — and any independent count behind the founder's price band. In particular, the claim that integration on one board was a **first** (C.1) has no adverse test anywhere in this corpus: nobody in the record checked the competing boards against it, and its only witness is the person with the strongest interest in its being true. The absence of contemporaneous failures in the print is a property of the source class — trade magazines printed advertisements and build logs, not return rates — and must not be read as evidence there were none.

## D. FIRST EXPERIMENT

STATUS: WRITTEN 2026-09-25

The stage definition this file defends (§STAGE BOUNDARY JUSTIFICATION) sets the terminal event as "the first meaningful real-world test of [the venture] — an artifact, a price, or an order that existed outside the founders' own assertion." This section reconstructs that test, and reports plainly that **two of the three named tokens are missing from the record**: the window contains an artifact, **no maker-printed price**, and **no documented order**.

### D.1 The dated ladder of the experiment, in-window only

| Date | Document | What it establishes | Class | Conf |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1976-04-30 | Sonoma County Micro Computer Club letter, reprinted in the Homebrew newsletter of that date: a working inventory of "several ALTAIR's, an IMSAI, a JOLT, two PDP-8's, **an APPLE** and some others on order"; "In April the APPLE 6502 system was our special guest. We are grateful to STEVE WOZNIAK for providing transportation." | The machine **existed, was named, and was in a third party's room**, 60 miles from its designer, before any commercial document. Read at the primary this pass (`sources/ia_homebrew/hcc0204.txt` l.140). L-3's ambiguity is preserved: "**on order**" may govern the APPLE, in which case April 1976 evidences a booking, not a delivery | CONTEMPORARY OBSERVATION, non-commercial, independently published | High (existence and naming); Medium (physical custody) |
| 1976-09 | Advertisement, Computer Mart of New York, 314 Fifth Avenue: "Take a byte out of the new Apple-1 com-puter" [sic, OCR line-break], on a page whose authorized-dealer list names Sphere, IMSAI, Processor Technology and SWTPC — **and does not name Apple** | The product was **advertised for sale by a retailer** five months after the club letter, in New York, not California. Re-read this pass at `sources/ia_byte_1976/byte-1976-09.txt` l.25277 | FACT (the advertisement exists as printed) | High. Identity of the advertiser triangulated in the same issue's distributor list (l.141–143), and "Computer Fan" is a probe misreading of a garbled logotype — **do not propagate it** (AP-14's superseding marker) |
| 1976-10, -11, -12 | The same New York retailer's brand block ("IMSAI, SWTPCo, Digital Group / Processor Tech, **Apple**, OSI / TDL-Z-80, Seals, Cromemco…") re-runs | **One campaign with a duration, not three witnesses.** December's list swaps Sphere for Veras, so the block is not stable month to month | FACT | High (printed) / independence: 1 origin |
| 1976-11 | Advertisement, Kentucky Fried Computers, 2465 Fourth Street, Berkeley: "\*Apple-Apple-1 computer" [sic] among "Starred items available by mail order at 10% discount from **manufacturer's current list prices** … Minimum order $80.00" | (i) A second, independently-owned seller. (ii) **A manufacturer's list price existed and is printed nowhere in the corpus.** (iii) The channel was mail order, not counter sale | FACT | High. AP-12's superseding marker adds the decisive context: the same firm told Homebrew readers on 1976-06-09 that it had **no store yet** and was "selling on a mail and telephone order basis" — so this is a storeless operation putting the Apple-1 on a mail-order price sheet |
| 1976-11 | BYTE technical article: "Several computers currently being marketed use such generalized hardware backed up by software in PROM monitors. These include the new **Apple computer**, BABY by STM Systems, etc." | An engineer's independent classification of Apple's machine as **marketed**, and of its architecture, within seven months of the partnership date | CONTEMPORARY OBSERVATION | High |
| 1976-11-20 | Helmers: "I first saw the Apple-ll on November 20 1976 when Stephen Wozniak and Stephen Jobs stopped by a motel room in Palo Alto where I was staying at the time. They brought along the prototype Apple-ll to give a demonstration." Then the three of them wrote a BASIC game in the machine's 5K interpreter "After perhaps 30 to 45 minutes" | The experiment had **already moved to its successor inside the window**: a prototype of the next machine was being demonstrated to the trade press's editor by both founders, three months before the window closes. Strongest single dated outside witness to either founder | CONTEMPORARY OBSERVATION, single participant-witness | Medium-High |
| 1976-12 | BYTE editorial names "Steven Jobs (Apple Computer Co)" among four "entrepeneurs"; the same issue's exhibitor list commits "**Apple Computers**" to the first West Coast Computer Faire alongside "Byte Shop of Palo Alto" | The firm exists as a named trade entity in maker-adjacent print; a founder is classified as an operator by an outside editor | CONTEMPORARY OBSERVATION | High (printed) |
| 1976-12-10 → 1977-01-19 | The two Homebrew issues straddling the incorporation carry **zero** body occurrences of the name, after five issues carrying it | The window's best outside primary source goes silent exactly across the legal event; BYTE's 1977 Apple coverage does not resume until April | FACT (the count) — **not** that the firm was inactive | High. C-20, recorded so nobody mistakes the April 1977 resumption for a January event |

### D.2 The first-customer question stays UNKNOWN, and here is what it does not stay

**UNKNOWN: the transaction.** The canonical first order — 50 Apple-1 boards at $500 each, July 1976, from Paul Terrell's Byte Shop, for resale at $666.66 — **has no document in this corpus**, and `research/D_adversarial.md` L-1 chased the lineage to its carriers and found: the designer's own 1977 sentence naming no buyer, no order, no unit count and no price; the Byte Shop's own 1976 advertising **never mentioning Apple** (across all twelve 1976 issues the token `Apple` occupies 13 lines, of which zero fall in a Byte Shop advertisement); and commercial carriers that admit resting on direct conversation. RR Auction's 2026 essay relies on talking to the merchant with no invoice, dated correspondence, printed volume or magazine entry anchoring the figures — **and dates the deal "Spring 1976", not July**. The apple-1-replica study gives "50 completed boards … 25,000 USD cash on delivery" citing a stamped manual cover plus a **2021 email reply** from Terrell. A rival price exists and is unadjudicated: a Tier-4 post attributed to Terrell himself, "sold Apple 1s to my Byte Shop owners for **$525** each and made $25 per unit". The 2026 Christie's essay restates the canon without citing a document (C-32). **One lineage, contradicting itself on date and on price.** No number from this cluster may appear as FACT in this file, in §F, §K, or the timeline (L-1's merge decision, adopted).

**Well documented: the relationship.** The separate proposition — that Terrell and the firm knew each other commercially in 1976 — is supported by three dated items, none of them memoir: Terrell and Jobs named as peers in one printed sentence after a stated September 1976 conversation on the WESCON floor; "Apple Computers" and "Byte Shop of Palo Alto" listed together on the same December 1976 exhibitor page; and Terrell's own 1976 Polaroids of the founders showing him the machine, published in 2022 through a collector registry (AP-30) — photographs whose **date attribution is the curator's**, not printed on the images as read here. The distinction is the section's load-bearing one: an early wholesale relationship is **very likely**; the units, price, date and payment terms are **undocumented** (U-C-4, U-AP-2).

**And one rung that the canon does not survive.** L-4 inverts the "first dealer" question entirely: because the New York advertiser enumerates the lines it is an **authorized dealer for** and Apple is not among them, and because the Berkeley advertiser claims no relationship either, **no 1976 document in the corpus evidences an authorized Apple dealer relationship of any kind**. That bars "first store", "first dealer" and "dealer network" as 1976 statements in *either* direction — the Byte-Shop-first story and its rivals — and is consistent with a partnership selling boards over the counter to whoever came.

### D.3 The tension that is this section's finding

Two artifacts in the local corpus describe the commercial machine far better than any in-window document does, and they are the two the brief names. **Both fall after the window closes** (see S1P1-CF-10), and their evidentiary use here is strictly as witnesses to the channel the window's product entered, not as Stage-1 state.

| Artifact | Date | What it prints | Why it is in tension |
|---|---|---|---|
| BYTE's national computer-store directory | 1977-04 | A state-by-state list of roughly **sixty named outlets across 22 jurisdictions including Canada and Japan**, of which about thirty-five are Byte Shop outlets, each with street address, telephone and a **named manager** — "Byte Shops, Inc., 1261 Birchwood Dr., Sunnyvale, CA 94086, (408) 734-9000, Bryan Kerr … Byte Shop of Tokyo, 2-9-9 Sotokanda, Chiyodaku, Tokyo" (A2-35) | The chain that memoir credits as Apple's founding customer is documented, in the month of the Apple II's announced introduction, as a **multi-state and trans-Pacific franchise with a named managing entity**. A "word of mouth" first customer is not nothing in that picture, but it is a very different claim from a one-shop relationship: the seller memoir names was itself a distribution machine, and the print gives it an enumerable circuit |
| Apple's own direct-mail order form | 1977-06, re-run 1977-07 | A nine-row price ladder (4K $1,298 system / $598 board-only up to 48K $2,638 / $1,938), California tax add-ons, aftermarket chip sets at $125 and $600, "we will include free a custom vinyl carrying case (a $50 value). And we will also pay shipping charges to anywhere in the continental United States", a mailing address, and a payment block: "□ BankAmericard □ VISA □ Master Charge" — the card boxes **re-read at the primary this pass**, `sources/ia_byte_1977/byte-1977-06.txt` l.2446–2448 (A2-29, B-35) | The founder's contemporaneous account of how the first product moved is "by word of mouth throughout California and later nationwide through retail computer stores". The maker's own first printed commercial instrument is **none of those things**: it is a national direct-mail operation, taking bank cards, paying the freight, and competing with its own resellers on terms — with a "dealer nearest you" telephone referral sitting on the same page (A2-27) |

**The finding, stated as a tension rather than resolved into a story.** L-10 adjudicates only the part the print can carry, and this file carries exactly that much: the founder's *sequence* is a compression — "California first, then nationwide, then retail" is contradicted in ordering by a New York advertisement in September 1976 and by the fact that the retail stage was running **before the maker advertised at all** (the maker's first advertisement is nine months later than the first dealer's). The *type* of channel Wozniak names — word of mouth, then generic retail, with **no founding order and no named buyer** — is corroborated by the strongest negative in the corpus: no 1976 document evidences any dealer agreement (L-4), Apple placed no advertisement of its own in 1976 (AP-22, A2-65), and no 1976 print states the price (A2-23). So the two best commercial artifacts do **not** refute the memoir; they **postdate its subject** and describe a different company, three months into corporationhood. What cannot be written either way is the transition itself: **no document in this corpus records how a word-of-mouth board sale became a bank-card mail-order business, and the mechanism is UNKNOWN.** The three candidate mechanisms — capital arriving with an outside investor, the Apple II making direct mailing viable, or a dealer network that formed between December 1976 and April 1977 — are all consistent with the print and none is evidenced by it. Naming one would be hindsight in a costume.

### D.4 Validation signals, per §7's validation line

| Date | Signal | Magnitude | What it demonstrated | What it did NOT demonstrate | Source | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1976-04-30 | A club outside the founder's county hosts the machine and names its designer | one letter, one club, ~60 miles | A working, demonstrable, named product with a designer willing to transport it himself | Demand, units, price, or that the club bought anything — "on order" is ambiguous (L-3) | `sources/ia_homebrew/hcc0204.txt`; A2-19; L-3 | Medium-High |
| 1976-09 → 1976-12 | One New York retailer advertises the product in four consecutive issues | 4 printings, **1 origin** | That a New York hobby shop judged an Apple-1 worth advertising to microcomputer buyers | A dealer relationship, an authorized line, volume sold, or Apple's involvement (L-4) | `sources/ia_byte_1976/byte-1976-09/-10/-11/-12.txt`; A2-08, A2-11, A2-17 | High (printed) / Low (anything inferential) |
| 1976-11 | A Berkeley mail-order firm discounts the Apple-1 off "manufacturer's current list prices" | 1 advertisement, 10% off, $80 minimum order | That a manufacturer's list price existed in November 1976 — the figure itself is **nowhere printed** | What the list price was; that the firm had stock; that Apple authorised it | AP-12 + superseding marker; A2-12, A2-39, A2-40 | High (printed) / UNKNOWN (price) |
| 1976-11 | A BYTE engineer classifies "the new Apple computer" among machines "currently being marketed" | 1 sentence | Independent technical classification of the product and its PROM-monitor architecture | Commercial scale, or that the writer had seen a sales figure | AP-13; A2-14 | High |
| 1976-11-20 | Successor prototype demonstrated to the trade editor by both founders, and programmed live | one session, ~30–45 minutes to a working BASIC game | That a working Apple-II prototype existed on a dated day, in both founders' custody, with a 5K BASIC and 16-bit integer arithmetic running | That anyone would buy one; that the demonstration converted to an order | A2-31, AP-15 | Medium-High |
| 1976-12 | Named as a committed Faire exhibitor alongside the Byte Shop; named among peers on the WESCON floor | 2 lists, 1 magazine each, 1 club | Standing inside the field — the firm is a category member that organisers list | Sales, capacity, or that the exhibitor commitment converted to a booth shown | AP-10, AP-11; A2-15, A2-16, B-40 | High (printed) |
| 1976 (whole year) | **Negative signal:** the maker placed no advertisement, printed no price, no address, no telephone, no letterhead | 0 documents, over 12 issues and 13 newsletters | That 1976 market presence ran through **other people's paper** — the load-bearing structural fact of the stage | Nothing about why (A2-65 names money, capacity and absence of literature as the three unevidenced candidates) | AP-22 + marker; A2-18, A2-65 | Medium-High |
| 1976 (whole year) | **Negative signal:** no unit count, no revenue, no order, no invoice exists | absent from every family searched | Nothing — it is a null, recorded so that later counts are not back-solved into it | Any production or sales figure whatsoever | AP-35; A2-23; D's L-9, L-11 | High |

**Boundary of the "200 Apple-1s" style of reasoning (L-9).** No count in any source may enter this section, because no in-window document counts units; the familiar "about 200 produced" traces to a 2011 Wikipedia retrieval via an aggregator. Any unit number is therefore **ESTIMATE with its derivation named, or UNKNOWN**, and it may not be used as a denominator in §L — which is where such figures usually smuggle in, to make an undocumented "50 boards" look like a majority of output.

**Anti-hagiography test (§2) applied to §D.** Read without knowing the ending, this section says: a hobbyist drove his own board to a club sixty miles away; two unrelated retailers, one of them in New York and one of them storeless, decided it was worth printing; an engineer filed it under machines being marketed; and its authors spent November 1976 showing a *different, unreleased* machine to a magazine editor in a motel. Sphere, IMSAI, Processor Technology, SWTPC, Cromemco and Vector Graphics sit in the same lists, some of them ahead of the Apple name, and several of them were better capitalised and further documented than Apple was in 1976. Nothing here reads as a company that was going to outlast them, because nothing in the record can say so.

**Record-selection null (§2), §D.** What a real first experiment would have generated and does not survive here: the order book, the invoice run, the bank relationship, the yield and failure rate on hand-built boards, the returns, the price list the Berkeley advertiser was discounting, and any count of what left the premises. The single object that could settle several of these — an **October 1976 Apple-1 advertisement image** naming a price (D's DG-D2) — is not in this corpus, and the title most likely to carry an Apple-I price advertisement, **Kilobaud 1976, has no machine-readable text layer at all** (AP-34; A2's DG-4). That is an intake condition, not a null about the world, and it is recorded as **UNTRIED** with its route below.

**UNTRIED (this part; 0 web requests, per brief).**
1. Kilobaud Sept–Dec 1976 OCR-locally for an Apple-1 price advertisement — route: `archive.org/metadata/Kilobaud197609` → JP2 → tesseract. Would settle the price question (D's DG-D2, A2's DG-4). **Not run: no document may be fetched this pass, and no OCR toolchain is in this agent's write scope.**
2. California SoS entity file for Apple Computer, Inc. — the only authority that can carry original 1977 charter text and any statement of information naming officers (C-01's route, C-07, C-13). Would settle the succession mechanics and the first officer question.
3. Christie's lot 242 clause transcription, and **Sotheby's New York lot 241 (2011-12-13)** — a separate catalogue of the same object fifteen years earlier, the most likely place to find transcribed clauses and images of both 1976 instruments (C-31's assigned research debt). Would settle Wayne's percentage and the capital-contribution terms.
4. Creative Computing and InfoWorld 1977–1981 IPO text (AP-34) — the only plausible printed carriers of FY1976/FY1977 fiscal columns. Would settle 1976 money or confirm the null across a fourth title.
5. Terrell's Polaroids read at image level, and any Santa Clara County assessor record for 20211 Crist Drive (AP-30, AP-36). Would date the first showing and settle premises.
